    me: yesterday you told me that I can programmatically detect if the addon equipmon has been loaded. Does that include the ability to tell if the grid is visible or not?

    No, programmatically checking if the equipmon addon is loaded does not tell you whether the actual equipment grid is visible on your screen.

    Knowing if the script is running vs. knowing if the UI elements are actively being drawn require two entirely different checks in Ashita v4.

    The Difference Between Loaded vs. VisibleLoaded Status:

        When you check if equipmon is loaded, you are just verifying that Ashita has compiled and executed the addon's Lua script files.

        Grid Visibility: equipmon can be fully loaded and active in the background while the grid itself is completely invisible to you. This happens if a user
        executes the slash command to toggle the display off, if the addon is temporarily hiding itself during cutscenes/events, or if its visibility variable is
        explicitly set to false in the configuration memory.

    How to Actually Track Visibility

    If you are writing a script or a LuaShitacast profile and need to know if the grid is actually rendered on screen, checking the addon list won't cut it. You will need to dig
    a layer deeper:

        Expose the Internal State: You would have to look into equipmon’s internal global configuration or state table (usually a variable like settings.visible or state.display).

        Cross-Addon Communication: Because Ashita addons run in isolated Lua environments, you cannot naturally read another addon's variables directly unless equipmon explicitly
        exports its visibility state to a shared global registry or provides a custom API property for other scripts to read.

    Are you trying to force the grid to show/hide at specific times in your macros, or are you trying to make another addon react conditionally when the grid disappears?

    me: I am developing a transparent grid overlay that will be marked with indications if a gear slot has been disabled, as a feedback mechanism. I can read equipmon's json settings
    for it's location so that my grid overlays the correct position and can handle the grid being moved. The reason I'm asking about visibility is that if the grid is not visible,
    an alternate display of the disabled slots will be needed. The overlay by itself has little meaning

    That makes total sense, and it is a clever way to handle visual feedback. Since you are building a companion UI to overlay equipmon, dealing with the visibility mismatch is a
    legitimate hurdle.

    Because equipmon relies on standard Ashita ImGui/font object rendering, and doesn't natively expose an API for external visibility checks, you have a couple of viable options
    to solve the visibility problem for your project:

    1. Intercept equipmon Commands via Packet/Command Hooks
        equipmon uses a specific toggle command—usually something like /equipmon show or /equipmon hide—to flip its layout state.

        The Solution: In your custom overlay addon, you can register a listener for the command event.

        How it works: Whenever your script intercepts the string /equipmon, read the arguments. If it detects the user typing /equipmon hide (or whatever toggle keyword it uses),
        your script can react simultaneously by setting your overlay's internal state to invisible and shifting to your alternate text/icon display mode.

    2. Monitor equipmon's Active Variables in Memory
        Because equipmon runs in an isolated Lua container, you normally can't reach inside it. However, Ashita v4 allows you to force-execute code within another addon's container
        using the /addon exec command.

        The Solution: You can poll equipmon for its active variables.

        How it works: Your addon can periodically call Ashita's execution bridge to force equipmon to print its current visibility state to a hidden file or pass it back, or you can
        write a tiny 2-line edit directly into equipmon's local file (equipmon.lua). By modifying its main rendering loop to export state.visible = true/false directly into Ashita's
        global registry environment (_G), your custom overlay script can instantly read that shared global variable on every frame.

    3. Check for the Ashita Cutscene Flag
        Often, the main reason an addon like equipmon becomes hidden without user interaction is because the player entered a cutscene, or the game's user interface has been entirely
        toggled off (like pressing Scroll Lock).

        The Solution: Check vanilla game state variables instead of the addon itself.

        How it works: You can check Ashita's memory mapping for AshitaCore:GetMemoryManager():GetEntity():GetLocalTarget() or check the specific game mode flags. If the game is currently
        rendering an event or cutscene, you can safely assume equipmon has automatically auto-hidden itself, allowing your overlay to match its behavior and shut off or pivot to the
        alternate UI layout.

    Which of these paths sounds best for your setup?

    me: I don't really like the idea of forcing equipmon to put it's visibility state into _G. I think a combination of intercepting the commands and parsing them along with recognizing
    when a cut scene or UI hiding event is the way to go. I can tell from the equipmon settings file whether on loading it starts visible or not.

    That approach is incredibly clean. By reading the initial state from the JSON configuration at launch, you establish a solid baseline without injecting messy global variables into _G.
    From there, handling text command hooks and tracking standard client state changes will cover nearly all your real-world scenarios.

    Here is how you can implement this logic inside your custom overlay addon for Ashita v4.

    1. Intercepting and Parsing /equipmon CommandsYou can register an ashita.events.register('command', ...) listener. By checking the command string, you can update your internal
    visibility boolean state before Ashita passes the command along to equipmon.

    -- Initialize using the value parsed from equipmon's JSON settings
    local is_equipmon_visible = true

    ashita.events.register('command', 'command_cb', function (cmd, nType)
        -- Break the command into lower-case arguments
        local args = cmd:lower():args()

        if #args > 0 and (args[1] == '/equipmon' or args[1] == '/eqm') then
            -- Handle explicit toggles based on equipmon's syntax
            if args[2] == 'hide' or args[2] == 'off' then
                is_equipmon_visible = false
            elseif args[2] == 'show' or args[2] == 'on' then
                is_equipmon_visible = true
            elseif args[2] == 'toggle' then
                is_equipmon_visible = not is_equipmon_visible
            end
        end

        -- Return false so we don't block the command from reaching equipmon
        return false
    end)

    2. Detecting Cutscenes & Hidden Vanilla UITo gracefully switch to your alternate display mode when a cutscene plays or when a player hits Scroll Lock to hide the UI, you should query
    the AshitaCore Memory Manager inside your addon's main rendering loop.

    local function should_render_overlay()
        -- 1. Check if the user manually hid the equipmon grid
        if not is_equipmon_visible then
            return false
        end

        -- 2. Check if the game vanilla UI is completely hidden (e.g., Scroll Lock)
        local gui = AshitaCore:GetMemoryManager():GetGui()
        if gui and gui:IsUiHidden() then
            return false
        end

        -- 3. Check if the player is currently stuck in an event or cutscene
        local pData = AshitaCore:GetMemoryManager():GetPlayer()
        if pData then
            -- FFXI event status flags (typically > 0 when inside an NPC cutscene)
            local status = pData:GetStatus()
            if status == 4 or status == 5 then -- 4 & 5 usually represent Event/Cutscene states
                return false
            end
        end

        return true
    end

    3. Tying It Together in Your Render LoopInside your main ImGui or Font rendering event, you can use the check to cleanly pivot your visual outputs:

    ashita.events.register('d3d_present', 'present_cb', function ()
        if should_render_overlay() then
            -- Draw your transparent grid indicators precisely on top of equipmon
            render_grid_overlay()
        else
            -- Draw your alternative warning list elsewhere on the screen
            render_alternate_disabled_slots_display()
        end
    end)
---
    me: I have a list of conditional codes (the buffs being one type.) I plan on making a jump table based on the whole conditional or a piece of the conditional in lua

    Using a jump table (dictionary of functions) is the absolute best, most performant way to handle conditional codes in Lua. Because Lua tables use highly optimized hash lookups, a jump
    table completely eliminates messy, slow if/elseif/else chains.

    When your conditional logic grows, you can structure your jump table to handle either the entire conditional or extract a piece of it (like an operation type or prefix).

    Here is a clean, scalable architectural pattern for doing this in Lua.

    local condition_handlers = {}

    -- Handler for checking active buffs (using your existing code!)
    condition_handlers["BUFF"] = function(payload)
        -- payload example: "BATTLE_SHOUT,PACK"
        -- Assuming buff_manager.has(input, bAll, bNot)
        return buff_manager.has(payload, false, false)
    end

    -- Handler for checking resources (e.g., Mana, Energy)
    condition_handlers["RESOURCE"] = function(payload)
        -- payload example: "MANA > 50"
        local type, operator, val = string.match(payload, "(%a+)%s*([<>=]+)%s*(%d+)")
        if type and operator and val then
            val = tonumber(val)
            local current = player.get_resource(type) -- Mock API
            if operator == ">" then return current > val end
            if operator == "<" then return current < val end
            if operator == "==" then return current == val end
        end
        return false
    end

    -- Handler for checking combat state
    condition_handlers["STATE"] = function(payload)
        -- payload example: "IN_COMBAT"
        if payload == "IN_COMBAT" then return player.in_combat() end
        if payload == "RESTING" then return player.is_resting() end
        return false
    end

    Step 2: The Evaluator (Parsing a Piece of the Conditional)

    If your conditionals look like "BUFF:BATTLE_SHOUT,PACK" or "RESOURCE:MANA>50", your main evaluator function can extract the prefix (the piece) to find the right function in the
    jump table, and pass the suffix (the payload) into it.

    local function evaluate_conditional(full_condition)
        if not full_condition or full_condition == "" then
            return false
        end

        -- 1. Split the string into a "Type" and a "Payload" using a delimiter like ":"
        -- Example: "BUFF:BATTLE_SHOUT,10" -> action_type = "BUFF", payload = "BATTLE_SHOUT,10"
        local action_type, payload = string.match(full_condition, "^([^:]+):(.*)$")

        if not action_type then
            -- Fallback: If there is no ":", maybe treat the whole string as a key
            action_type = full_condition
            payload = ""
        end

        action_type = action_type:upper() -- Keep uppercase consistent

        -- 2. Jump Table Lookup
        local handler = condition_handlers[action_type]

        if handler then
            -- Run the matched function and return its true/false result
            return handler(payload)
        else
            reporting.DisplayOnce("Warning: Unknown conditional type '" .. action_type .. "'", false)
            return false
        end
    end

    Why this pattern is incredibly powerful in Lua:

    Infinite Scalability: If you decide to add a new system next week (e.g., checking item cooldowns COOLDOWN:HEAL_POTION), you do not touch your evaluator loop or main codebase.
    You simply add condition_handlers["COOLDOWN"] = function(payload) ... end to your table.

    Handling the Whole Conditional: If you want a specific condition to map 1:1 without arguments, you can just map the whole string directly into the table:

    condition_handlers["IS_DEAD"] = function() return player.is_dead() end

    Speed: Lua hashes keys instantly. Looking up a handler among 100 choices happens in near-constant time (O(1)), whereas 100 elseif blocks force Lua to evaluate line-by-line
    sequentially.

    Are your conditional strings formatted with a specific prefix delimiter (like BUFF:xyz or BUFF_xyz), or are you processing them as nested tables instead of strings?

    me: the conditionals that contain a colon should be split. The piece before the colon identifies the type, the piece after the colon is the key

    Here is exactly how to structure your string-splitting evaluator to extract the type (before the colon) and the key (after the colon), while safely routing them through
    your jump table.

    -- 1. Your Jump Table containing the functions for each type

    local conditional_jump_table = {
        BUFF = function(key)
            -- 'key' will be things like "BATTLE_SHOUT,PACK" or "10,cat"
            return buff_manager.has(key, false, false)
        end,

        COOLDOWN = function(key)
            -- 'key' might be "HEAL_POTION" or "1025"
            return cooldown_manager.is_ready(key)
        end,

        TALENT = function(key)
            return talent_manager.has_learned(key)
        end
    }

    -- 2. The Evaluator Function
    local function evaluate_condition(condition_string)
        if not condition_string or condition_string == "" then
            return false
        end

        local cond_type, cond_key

        -- Check if it contains a colon
        if string.find(condition_string, ":") then
            -- Split by the first colon found
            -- %s* handles optional spaces around the colon like "BUFF : dog"
            cond_type, cond_key = string.match(condition_string, "^%s*([^:]+)%s*:%s*(.*)$")
        else
            -- If no colon, treat the whole string as the type (with an empty key)
            cond_type = condition_string
            cond_key = ""
        end

        -- Clean the type up so it safely matches your jump table keys
        if cond_type then
            cond_type = utilities.fTrim(cond_type):upper()
        end

        -- 3. Look up and execute via the Jump Table
        local handler = conditional_jump_table[cond_type]
        if handler then
            return handler(cond_key)
        else
            reporting.DisplayOnce("Warning: Unknown conditional type: " .. tostring(cond_type), false)
            return false
        end
    end

    How the Pattern Handles Different Formats
                                cond_type       cond key
    Input String                extracted       to handler      Behavior
    ------------------------    -----------     ----------      ----------------------------------------------------------
    "BUFF:10,dog"               "BUFF"          "10,dog"        Calls the BUFF function, passing "10,dog" to your buff_manager.
    "cooldown : HEAL_POTION"    "COOLDOWN"      "HEAL_POTION"   Cleans up the extra spaces and casing automatically.
    "IS_ALIVE"                  "IS_ALIVE"      ""              Handles inputs without a colon gracefully by checking the whole string.

    Why this specific string.match pattern is safe:

    The pattern "^%s*([^:]+)%s*:%s*(.*)$" works perfectly because it stops at the very first colon. If your key contains additional colons later on for some reason
    (e.g., TYPE:KEY:SUBKEY), it won't break—the system will safely capture everything after the first colon entirely as the cond_key.

    me: each of my conditionals, when evaluated, will return true or false

    That makes the execution architecture incredibly elegant. Because every handler in your jump table guarantees a boolean (true or false) return value, you can chain
    these conditional strings together using clean logic.

    Here is how you wrap your jump table execution to process multiple conditional expressions seamlessly.

    Step 1: The Jump Table Configuration

        Each entry is perfectly uniform, executing its specific logic and returning a strict boolean.

        local conditional_jump_table = {
            BUFF = function(key)
                -- Evaluates presence of buffs, returning a boolean
                return buff_manager.has(key, false, false)
            end,

        COOLDOWN = function(key)
            -- Returns true if the action/spell is off cooldown
            return cooldown_manager.is_ready(key)
            end,

        COMBAT = function(key)
            -- Returns true if state matches player state (e.g., key = "true" or "false")
            local required_state = (key:lower() == "true")
            return player.in_combat() == required_state
            end
        }

    Step 2: Evaluating a Single Condition String

        This helper parses the colon, executes the matching jump table function, and hands back the boolean outcome.

        local function evaluate_single_condition(condition_string)
            if not condition_string or condition_string == "" then
                return false
            end

            local cond_type, cond_key

            if string.find(condition_string, ":") then
                cond_type, cond_key = string.match(condition_string, "^%s*([^:]+)%s*:%s*(.*)$")
            else
                cond_type = condition_string
                cond_key = ""
            end

            if cond_type then
                cond_type = utilities.fTrim(cond_type):upper()
            end

            local handler = conditional_jump_table[cond_type]
            if handler then
                -- This guarantees a strict true or false return
                return handler(cond_key) == true
            else
                reporting.DisplayOnce("Warning: Unknown conditional type: " .. tostring(cond_type), false)
                return false
            end
        end

    Step 3: Combining Conditionals (AND / OR chains)

        Since each evaluation returns a plain boolean, you can evaluate lists of conditionals using standard loop properties.

        Scenario A: If ALL conditionals must pass (Implicit "AND")

        If you pass a table or a comma-delimited sequence of distinct conditionals, you can loop through them. The moment any single condition returns false,
        you break early and return false.

        local function evaluate_all_conditions(condition_list)
            -- Expects a Lua array of strings, e.g., {"BUFF:10,dog", "COOLDOWN:HEAL_POTION"}
            for i = 1, #condition_list do
                if not evaluate_single_condition(condition_list[i]) then
                    return false -- Early exit: One constraint failed
                end
            end
            return true -- Everything passed!
        end

        Scenario B: If AT LEAST ONE conditional must pass (Implicit "OR")

        local function evaluate_any_condition(condition_list)
            for i = 1, #condition_list do
                if evaluate_single_condition(condition_list[i]) then
                    return true -- Early exit: We found a match
                end
            end
            return false -- None of them were true

    Are you storing these conditional strings as a flat array/table inside your engine configuration, or are you planning to pass them as another comma-delimited
    string (e.g., "BUFF:dog, COOLDOWN:spell") that needs to be broken apart first?

    me: the conditionals are an extra check as to whether a piece of gear should be equipped in luashitacast. They ask a question that can be either true or false. If true,
    the conditional has been met. if false the piece of gear they're attached to will be skipped

    Knowing this is for Luashitacast (the highly popular gear-swapping script engine for Final Fantasy XI private servers) makes the goal crystal clear!

    In Luashitacast, gear swaps happen incredibly fast during precast, midcast, and aftercast phases. Your conditional check serves as a gatekeeper: if a condition evaluates
    to false, the script ignores that specific piece of gear (or entire set adjustment) and moves on.

    Because Luashitacast relies on split-second execution to prevent macro lag, a jump table structure is perfect here. It avoids massive if/elseif chains during heavy combat.

    Integrating the Jump Table into your Cast File

    Here is how you can naturally structure this within your Luashitacast script to evaluate whether a gear slot or set should be equipped:

    -- 1. The Jump Table (Defined globally or at the top of your profile)
        local conditional_checks = {
            -- Checks if specific buffs are active (e.g., "BUFF:10,dog")
            BUFF = function(key)
                -- Calls your robust buff manager function
                return buff_manager.has(key, false, false)
            end,

            -- Checks if a specific spell or ability is off cooldown
            COOLDOWN = function(key)
                -- Mocking a recast check: returns true if ready
                local id = tonumber(key) or spell_to_id_lookup[key:upper()]
                return ashita.ffxi.recast.get_spell_recast(id) == 0
            end,

            -- Checks player status (e.g., "STATUS:Engaged" or "STATUS:Idle")
            STATUS = function(key)
                local current_status = gData.GetPlayer().Status
                return current_status:lower() == key:lower()
            end,

            -- Checks if player TP meets a threshold (e.g., "TP:>1000")
            TP = function(key)
                local operator, amount = string.match(key, "^([<>=]+)%s*(%d+)$")
                if not operator then return false end

                local current_tp = gData.GetPlayer().TP
                amount = tonumber(amount)

                if operator == ">" then return current_tp > amount end
                if operator == "<" then return current_tp < amount end
                return false
            end
        }

        -- 2. The Core Evaluator Function
        local function clear_to_equip(condition_string)
            if not condition_string or condition_string == "" then
                return true -- No condition attached means it's always safe to equip
            end

            local cond_type, cond_key
            if string.find(condition_string, ":") then
                cond_type, cond_key = string.match(condition_string, "^%s*([^:]+)%s*:%s*(.*)$")
            else
                cond_type = condition_string
                cond_key = ""
            end

            cond_type = cond_type:trim():upper() -- Luashitacast environment usually includes string helpers like :trim()

            local handler = conditional_checks[cond_type]
            if handler then
                -- Returns true (equip it!) or false (skip it!)
                return handler(cond_key) == true
            else
                -- Unknown condition fails safely and skips the gear
                return false
            end
        end

    How to use this inside Luashitacast rules (gFunc.HandlePrecast, etc.)

    You can map your gear choices or sets alongside their validation strings. Here are two ways you can implement this in your profile:

    Method A: Filtering an entire swap inline

    function gFunc.HandlePrecast(spell)
        if spell.Skill == 'Healing Magic' then
            -- Only equip the high-tier MP conservation piece if our condition is true
            if clear_to_equip("BUFF:BATTLE_SHOUT,PACK") then
                gFunc.Equip('Body', 'Aristocrat\'s Coat')
            else
                gFunc.Equip('Body', 'Cure Coat') -- Fallback piece
            end
        end
    end

    Method B: A data-driven approach for a rules table

    If you have a customized table defining your gear setups, you can bind the strings directly to them:

    local situational_gear = {
        { item = "Refresh Ring", condition = "BUFF:Refresh" },
        { item = "Defending Ring", condition = "STATUS:Engaged" },
    }

    -- Inside your routine:
    for _, gear_rule in ipairs(situational_gear) do
        if clear_to_equip(gear_rule.condition) then
            gFunc.Equip('Ring1', gear_rule.item)
        end
    end

---
    me: Is there a better way to define what conditional is referenced in the table than cascading attempts? For example, try the whole conditional. If it matches, great. If not, can I split the
    conditional into a keyword and value looking for colon. Try the keyword, etc

    Yes, there is a significantly better way than cascading attempts. Cascading if/else checks or string splits at runtime completely defeats the performance benefits of using a jump table.

    The optimal approach is to pre-compile or parse the condition once when the addon loads, transforming the string key into a pre-structured object. At runtime during gear swaps, you perform
    a direct, single-step lookup.

    Here is how to implement a clean, high-performance lookup system using pre-parsed conditions.

        1. The Pre-Parsed Jump Table Structure

        Instead of expecting the jump table to handle raw strings like "NOT_Buff:Stun", you separate the condition logic into a Type (the function) and a Value (the argument passed to it),
        while handling the NOT_ inversion uniformly.

        -- Core logical building blocks
        local condition_types = {
            ["Buff"] = function(buff_name)
                return gData.GetBuffCount(buff_name) > 0
            end,
            ["TP_Less"] = function(amount)
                return player.tp < tonumber(amount)
            end,
            ["DayElement"] = function(element)
                return world.day_element == element
            end
        }

        2. The Pre-Parser (Run Once at Load)

        You write a helper function that reads a raw conditional string and compiles it into an efficient data structure. This happens only once when your script or profile initializes, completely
        eliminating string parsing during heavy mid-cast/pre-cast combat events.

        local function compile_condition(condition_str)
            if not condition_str or condition_str == "" then return nil end

            local is_inverted = false
            local working_str = condition_str

            -- 1. Check and strip NOT_ prefix
            if string.sub(working_str, 1, 4) == "NOT_" then
                is_inverted = true
                working_str = string.sub(working_str, 5)
            end

            -- 2. Split into keyword and value by colon (if it exists)
            local keyword, value = working_str:match("([^:]+):?(.*)")

            -- Get the base evaluation function
            local eval_func = condition_types[keyword]
            if not eval_func then
                -- Fallback if the keyword doesn't exist
                return function() return false end
            end

            -- 3. Return a highly optimized, self-contained closure function
            return function()
                -- Evaluate the base condition using the pre-parsed value argument
                local result = eval_func(value ~= "" and value or nil)

                -- Apply the inversion logic instantly
                return is_inverted and not result or result
            end
        end

        3. Initialize Your Gear Sets

        When your addon loads, you loop through your gear sets and swap out the raw strings for these pre-compiled closures.

        local function initialize_sets()
            for set_name, gear_set in pairs(sets) do
                for slot, gear_data in pairs(gear_set) do
                    if type(gear_data) == "table" and gear_data.condition then
                        -- Replace the raw string string with the optimized function
                        gear_data.eval = compile_condition(gear_data.condition)
                    end
                end
            end
        end

        -- Call this once at the bottom of your script/profile load
        initialize_sets()

        4. The Lightning-Fast Runtime Execution

        Because the string parsing, colon splitting, and NOT_ checking were all completed at load time, your runtime loop becomes incredibly short, clean, and fast.

        local function resolve_dynamic_set(set_name)
            local base_set = sets[set_name]
            if not base_set then return {} end

            local parsed_set = {}

            for slot, gear_data in pairs(base_set) do
                -- Direct evaluation: No string manipulation, no cascading attempts
                if type(gear_data) == "table" and gear_data.eval then
                    parsed_set[slot] = gear_data.eval() and gear_data.alt or gear_data.base
                else
                    parsed_set[slot] = gear_data
                end
            end

            return parsed_set
        end

    Why This Method ExcelsTrue O(1) Execution: At runtime, the code simply executes gear_data.eval(). Lua executes closures incredibly fast, meaning your gear swaps will consume near-zero
    CPU cycles during battle.

    Ultimate Flexibility: You can write complex conditions like "NOT_Buff:Sneak Attack" or "DayElement:Light". The pre-parser smoothly dissects them into their constituent parts before the
    game even finishes loading, leaving you with a perfectly clean jump table.

    To ensure this fits seamlessly into your addon, could you share:

    What environment or structure you are using to store your user sets (e.g., standard Luashitacast .lua profiles, or a separate JSON/XML config file)?
    A few examples of your exact colon-separated keywords (like Buff:Stun) so we can verify the pre-parser matches your exact syntax rules?

    me: So, you do a pre-pass through all the gearsets looking for conditionals and effectively come up with translations for all of the conditionals. That way when a conditional is hit at
    runtime the breakdown is already known

    Exactly! You nailed the core concept. By doing that pre-pass, you change the entire architecture from runtime parsing (slow, repetitive) to runtime execution (near-instant).

    Instead of forcing your CPU to shred strings mid-combat while you are trying to cast a spell, your addon does all the heavy lifting exactly once when the profile loads.

    Here is exactly what that translation looks like under the hood.

    The "Before and After" Translation

    When Luashitacast first boots up, it looks at your raw, human-readable gear set:

    -- BEFORE THE PRE-PASS (Human-Readable)
    Waist = {
        base = "Eschan Stone",
        condition = "NOT_Buff:Stun",
        alt = "Hachirin-no-Obi"
    }

    The pre-pass scans the set, isolates "NOT_Buff:Stun", dissects it, and injects a hidden, pre-compiled function (eval) directly into that piece of gear.

    Once the pre-pass finishes, the gear entry in memory effectively looks like this:

    -- AFTER THE PRE-PASS (Machine-Optimized)
    Waist = {
        base = "Eschan Stone",
        condition = "NOT_Buff:Stun",
        alt = "Hachirin-no-Obi",

        -- The translation pre-packages the logic, the 'NOT_', and the 'Stun' argument
        eval = function()
            local has_stun = gData.GetBuffCount("Stun") > 0
            return not has_stun
        end
    }

    The Runtime Reality

    When you actually cast a spell and Luashitacast triggers your gear swap, the runtime code doesn't care how complex the condition string originally was. It doesn't see colons, prefixes,
    or keywords. It just executes the pre-packaged function instantly:

    -- Lightning fast runtime check
    if gear_data.eval() then
        equip(gear_data.alt)
    else
        equip(gear_data.base)
    end

    Taking it a Step Further: Memorization (Caching)

    If you want to make this even faster, you can add memorization to your translation loop. If five different gear sets all use "NOT_Buff:Stun", you don't even need to build five different functions.
    You can cache the translation the first time you see it:

    local compiled_cache = {}

    local function compile_condition(condition_str)
        if not condition_str or condition_str == "" then return nil end

        -- If we already translated this exact string earlier, reuse it!
        if compiled_cache[condition_str] then
            return compiled_cache[condition_str]
        end

        -- ... (The parsing logic from before goes here) ...

        local final_closure = function()
            return is_inverted and not eval_func(value) or eval_func(value)
        end

        -- Save it to the cache before returning
        compiled_cache[condition_str] = final_closure
        return final_closure
    end

    By adding a cache, your pre-pass is incredibly lean, and your runtime execution is as close to hardware-level speed as Lua can possibly get.

    me: So would you have an entry in the jump table that matches the complete conditional? How it's processed would be done in the prepass

    Yes, exactly. The jump table itself changes roles based on this approach:

    During the Pre-Pass: The jump table acts as your factory lookup. You look up the base keyword (like Buff or TP_Less) to find the factory function that builds the condition checker.

    After the Pre-Pass (At Runtime): You don't use that base jump table anymore. Instead, your compiled condition cache becomes the ultimate runtime jump table, where the keys are the exact,
    complete conditional strings and the values are the highly optimized functions.

    By mapping the complete, raw conditional string directly to its fully prepared function in a cache, the runtime lookup takes literally one step.Here is how that workflow looks in code:

    1. The Pre-Pass "Factory" Jump Table

    This table only knows how to handle the base mechanics. It doesn't care about NOT_ prefixes or specific arguments yet.

    local condition_factories = {
    ["Buff"] = function(buff_name)
        return function() return gData.GetBuffCount(buff_name) > 0 end
    end,
    ["TP_Less"] = function(amount)
        local tp_val = tonumber(amount) or 0
        return function() return player.tp < tp_val end
    end,
    ["Status"] = function(status_name)
        return function() return player.status == status_name end
    end
    }

    2. The Runtime Jump Table (The Compiled Cache)

    As the pre-pass runs, it fills a secondary table (compiled_conditionals). This table will match your complete, exact strings to their final execution logic.

    -- This will become your blazing-fast runtime jump table
    local compiled_conditionals = {}

    local function prepare_conditional(full_condition_str)
        -- If we've already processed this exact string, skip the work!
        if compiled_conditionals[full_condition_str] then
            return compiled_conditionals[full_condition_str]
        end

        local is_inverted = false
        local working_str = full_condition_str

        -- Strip 'NOT_' if present
        if string.sub(working_str, 1, 4) == "NOT_" then
            is_inverted = true
            working_str = string.sub(working_str, 5)
        end

        -- Split keyword and value (e.g., "Buff" and "Stun")
        local keyword, value = working_str:match("([^:]+):?(.*)")

        local factory = condition_factories[keyword]
        if not factory then
            -- Fallback for unhandled/typoed strings
            return function() return false end
        end

        -- Generate the core evaluation function for this specific value
        local base_eval = factory(value ~= "" and value or nil)

        -- Wrap it to handle the potential inversion
        local final_runtime_func = function()
            local res = base_eval()
            return is_inverted and not res or res
        end

        -- Save the complete string to our runtime jump table
        compiled_conditionals[full_condition_str] = final_runtime_func
        return final_runtime_func
    end

    How the Pre-Pass Uses It

    When loading your gear sets, you run through them and link the gear definition straight to the runtime jump table via the complete string:

    for set_name, gear_set in pairs(sets) do
        for slot, gear_data in pairs(gear_set) do
            if type(gear_data) == "table" and gear_data.condition then
                -- This populates and links to the complete-string jump table
                gear_data.eval = prepare_conditional(gear_data.condition)
            end
        end
    end

    The Ultimate Runtime Performance

    When Luashitacast triggers a gear swap mid-combat, the runtime process is down to a single, direct function execution. It never has to split a colon,
    look up a keyword, or evaluate a string prefix again:

    -- Inside your runtime gear resolver:
    if gear_data.eval() then
        return gear_data.alt
    else
        return gear_data.base
    end

    me: in some ways you're doing the cascading I was talking about, but only to define jump table entries and only doing it once. Interesting

    Exactly! You hit the nail on the head.

    The strategy shifts the cost of that complex cascading logic entirely. Instead of paying a CPU tax hundreds of times a minute during intense combat frames, you pay that tax exactly once for a fraction
    of a millisecond when the addon boots up.

    At runtime, all that conditional complexity is completely flattened into a simple, direct pointer execution.

    Extending to Complex Combinations

    Because this cascading breakdown happens entirely during initialization, you can expand it to handle incredibly advanced logic—like combining multiple conditions using AND (&) or OR (|) operators—without
    adding any performance drag to your live gear swaps.

    Here is how you can handle multi-conditional strings like "Buff:Sneak Attack&Status:Engaged" in the pre-pass:

    -- Helper to split strings by a delimiter during the pre-pass
    local function split_string(input, sep)
        local t = {}
        for str in string.gmatch(input, "([^" .. sep .. "]+)") do
            table.insert(t, str)
        end
        return t
    end

    -- The Pre-Parser compiler now handles compound 'AND' strings
    local function prepare_conditional(full_condition_str)
        if compiled_conditionals[full_condition_str] then
            return compiled_conditionals[full_condition_str]
        end

        -- Handle compound 'AND' conditions (e.g., "CondA&CondB")
        if string.find(full_condition_str, "&") then
            local sub_conditions = split_string(full_condition_str, "&")
            local compiled_subs = {}

            -- Recursively compile each individual condition
            for _, sub_str in ipairs(sub_conditions) do
                table.insert(compiled_subs, prepare_conditional(sub_str))
            end

            -- Create a compound runtime closure
            local compound_func = function()
                for _, eval_sub in ipairs(compiled_subs) do
                    if not eval_sub() then return false end -- Short-circuit out on first failure
                end
                return true
            end

            compiled_conditionals[full_condition_str] = compound_func
            return compound_func
        end

        -- --- [Your original single-condition logic continues here] ---
        -- (Strip NOT_, match keyword:value, look up factory, etc.)
    end

    The End Result in Memory

    If a user configures a piece of gear with a highly intricate string:

    Waist = {
        base = "Eschan Stone",
        condition = "NOT_Buff:Amnesia&Status:Engaged",
        alt = "Windbuffet Belt"
    }

    The pre-pass does its cascading work, recursively breaks down the operators, strips prefixes, finds the factories, and hands Luashitacast a single compiled runtime function. When you attack a
    monster, the execution environment evaluates it with maximum efficiency:

    -- The structural reality at runtime:
    gear_data.eval = function()
        -- Check sub-condition 1 (Inverted Buff check)
        if not (not (gData.GetBuffCount("Amnesia") > 0)) then return false end
        -- Check sub-condition 2 (Status check)
        if not (player.status == "Engaged") then return false end

        return true
    end

    By embracing the architectural principle of doing it once at load time, your code remains beautiful and legible to write, while executing at near-compiled machine speeds.

    me: so, I could have a jump table with generic forms and then in the preprocessing, copy over the generic form with tailored specifics to the actual jump table for all entries during the preprocess

    Yes, exactly! You have perfectly deduced the cleanest way to implement this factory pattern in Lua.

    Your master "generic form" table contains the blueprint templates, and during the preprocess, you generate a tailored instance of that blueprint using the exact arguments (like Stun or 1000) and the NOT_
    inversion rule. This tailored instance is what gets written directly into your active runtime jump table.

    In Lua, this is incredibly efficient because you can use closures (functions that "remember" the variables around them when they were created) to lock in those tailored specifics.

    Here is exactly how that blueprint copying and tailoring looks in practice:

    local generic_blueprints = {
        ["Buff"] = function(target_buff)
            -- This inner function is tailored; it locks in 'target_buff' forever
            return function()
                return gData.GetBuffCount(target_buff) > 0
            end
        end,

        ["TP_Greater"] = function(target_tp)
            local tp_cutoff = tonumber(target_tp) or 0
            return function()
                return player.tp >= tp_cutoff
            end
        end
    }

    2. The Tailoring Step (The Preprocess Copy)

    When your prescanner finds a conditional string, it copies the blueprint, injects the tailored specifics, and saves the result into your runtime jump table (runtime_jump_table).

    -- The actual jump table used at runtime
    local runtime_jump_table = {}

    local function tailor_and_register(full_condition_str)
        -- If this exact variation was already tailored, don't recreate it
        if runtime_jump_table[full_condition_str] then
            return runtime_jump_table[full_condition_str]
        end

        local is_inverted = false
        local working_str = full_condition_str

        -- 1. Handle the NOT_ prefix tailoring
        if string.sub(working_str, 1, 4) == "NOT_" then
            is_inverted = true
            working_str = string.sub(working_str, 5)
        end

        -- 2. Extract the keyword and the specific value argument
        local keyword, specific_value = working_str:match("([^:]+):?(.*)")

        local blueprint = generic_blueprints[keyword]
        if not blueprint then
            -- Safe fallback if the user made a typo in the keyword
            runtime_jump_table[full_condition_str] = function() return false end
            return runtime_jump_table[full_condition_str]
        end

        -- 3. Copy the generic form and bake in the tailored specific value
        local tailored_base_func = blueprint(specific_value ~= "" and specific_value or nil)

        -- 4. Apply the final logical inversion layer if NOT_ was present
        local final_tailored_func = function()
            local result = tailored_base_func()
            return is_inverted and not result or result
        end

        -- 5. Store this perfectly tailored copy into the runtime jump table
        runtime_jump_table[full_condition_str] = final_tailored_func
        return final_tailored_func
    end

    3. Integrating with Your Existing Prescan

    Because you are already doing a prescan loop, you just pass the string to the tailor, and attach the resulting optimized function pointer directly to the gear asset:

    -- Inside your existing prescan loop:
    if type(gear_data) == "table" and gear_data.condition then
        -- Tailor the generic blueprint and attach it as a direct function reference
        gear_data.eval = tailor_and_register(gear_data.condition)
    end

    The Runtime Lookup is Now a Single Step

    Because the runtime table is populated with keys matching the exact full condition strings, your gear resolution system bypasses all processing entirely.

    -- At runtime, it's just a direct execution of the tailored function pointer
    if gear_data.eval() then
        return gear_data.alt
    else
        return gear_data.base
    end

    By using this architecture, your addon maintains absolute separation of concerns: your blueprints stay clean and generic, your initialization code handles the complexity, and your runtime
    gear swapper stays spectacularly fast.
